<span  style="font-size: 20.0px;">arXiv:2204.08882v4  [q-fin.RM]  29 Jun 2023</span>

<span  style="font-size: 17.21540069580078px;">Calibrating distribution models from PELVE</span>

<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Hirbod Assa</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Liyuan Lin</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Ruodu Wang</span>

<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">June 30, 2023</span>













<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Keywords:</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Value-at-Risk, Expected Shortfall, risk measures, heavy tails, advanced diﬀerential</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">equation.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Introduction</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Value-at-Risk (VaR) and Expected Shortfall (ES, also known as TVaR and CVaR) are the most</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">widely used risk measures for regulation in ﬁnance and insurance. The former has gained its popularity</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">due to its simplistic approach toward risk as the risk quantile, and the second one is perceived to be use-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ful as a modiﬁcation of VaR with more appealing properties, such as tail-sensitivity and subadditivity,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">as studied in the seminal work of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Artzner et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1999</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In the Fundamental Review of the Trading Book (FRTB), the Basel Committee on Banking Su-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">pervision (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">BCBS</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2019</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) proposed to replace VaR at 1% conﬁdence with ES with a 2.5% conﬁdence</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">interval for the internal model-based approach.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The main reason, as mentioned in the FRTB, was that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES can better capture tail risk; see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2018</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for a concrete risk sharing model where tail</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">risk is captured by ES and ignored by VaR. On the other hand, VaR also has advantages that ES does</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">not have, such as elicitability (e.g.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Gneiting</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2011</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Kou and Peng</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2016</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) or backtesting tractabil-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ity (e.g.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Acerbi and Sz´ekely</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2014</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)), and the two risk measures admit diﬀerent axiomatic foundations</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Chambers</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2009</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Wang and Zitikis</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2021</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)). We refer to the reviews of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2014</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Emmer et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2015</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for general discussions on VaR and ES, and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> McNeil et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2015</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for a stan-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">dard treatment on risk management including the use of VaR and ES. The technical contrasts of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">two risk measures and their co-existence in regulatory practice give rise to great interest from both</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">researchers and practitioners to explore the relationship between them.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">To understand the balancing point of VaR and ES during the transition in the FRTB,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) proposed the Probability Equivalent Level of VaR-ES (PELVE). The value of PELVE is the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">multiplier to the tail probability when replacing VaR with ES such that the capital calculation stays</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">unchanged. More precisely, the PELVE of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> at level</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the multiplier</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">);</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">such</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> uniquely exists under mild conditions. For instance, if VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for a future</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">portfolio loss</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, then PELVE of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> at probability level 0.01 is the multiplier 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5. In this case, replacing</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> in FRTB does not have much eﬀect on the capital requirement for the bank bearing</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the loss</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Instead, if ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), then the bank has a larger capital requirement under</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the new regulatory risk measure; this is often the case for ﬁnancial assets and portfolios as shown by</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the empirical studies in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). The PELVE enjoys many convenient properties, and it</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">has been extended in a few ways. In particular,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Fiori and Rosazza Gianin</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2023</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) deﬁned generalized</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE by replacing VaR and ES with another pair of monotone risk measures (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ρ,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ˜</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ρ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Barczy et al.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) extended PELVE by replacing ES with a higher-order ES.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For a given distribution model or a data set, its PELVE can be computed or estimated in a</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">straightforward manner. As argued by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), the PELVE for a small</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> may be seen</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">as a summarizing index measuring tail heaviness in a non-limit sense.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As such, one may like to</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">generate models for a given PELVE, in a way similar to constructing models for other given statistical</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">information; see e.g.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2002</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2016</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for constructing multivariate models with a given</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">correlation or tail-dependence matrix. Such statistical information may be obtained either from data</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">or from expert opinion, but there is no a priori guarantee that a corresponding model exists. Since</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE involves a parameter</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1), its information is represented by a curve. The calibration</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">problem, that is, to ﬁnd a distribution model for given PELVE values or a given PELVE curve, turns</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">out to be highly non-trivial, and it is the main objective of the current paper.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">From now on, suppose that we receive some information on the PELVE of a certain random</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">loss from an expert opinion, and we aim to build a distribution model consistent with the supplied</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">information. Since PELVE is location-scale invariant, such a distribution, if it exists, is not unique.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The calibration problem is trivial if we are supplied with only one point on the PELVE curve.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As the PELVE curve of the generalized Pareto distribution is a constant when the PELVE is well</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">deﬁned, we can use the generalized Pareto distribution to match the given PELVE value, which has</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a tail index implied from the expert opinion. The calibration problem becomes more involved if we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">are supplied with two points on the PELVE curve, because the value of the PELVE at two diﬀerent</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">probability levels interact with each other. The situation becomes more complicated as the number of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">points increases, and we further turn to the problem of calibration from a fully speciﬁed PELVE curve.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Calibrating distribution from the PELVE curve can be reformulated as solving for a function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> via the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">integral equation</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> yf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), where the curve</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is computed from the PELVE curve. This</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">integral equation can be further converted to an advanced diﬀerential equation (see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Bellman and Cooke</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1963</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)). For the case that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a constant curve, we can explicitly obtain all solutions for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We ﬁnd</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">other distributions that also have constant PELVE curves other than the simple ones with a Pareto or</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">exponential distribution. As a consequence, a PELVE curve does not characterize a unique location-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">scale family of distributions; this provides a negative answer to a question posed by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Section 7, Question (iv)). For general function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we develop a numerical method to compute</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The calibrated distribution can be used to estimate the value of other risk measures such as VaR</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and ES at diﬀerent levels. We illustrate by an empirical example that two points of PELVE give a</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">quite good summary of the tail distribution of risk. Daily log-losses (negative log-returns) of Apple</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(AAPL) from Yahoo Finance are collected for the period from January 3, 2012 to December 31, 2021</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">within total of 2518 observations. We calculate the empirical PELVE at levels 0.01 and 0.05 using the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">empirical PELVE estimator provided by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Section 5) with a moving window of 500</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">trading days. For each pair of two points of PELVE at levels 0.01 and 0.05, we produce a quantile</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">curve from the two empirical PELVE points by our calibration model in Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3.2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which is scaled</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">such that VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are equal to their empirical values.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> presents the empirical and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">calibrated quantile curves on December 31, 2021 using 500 trading days prior to that date. The two</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">quantile curves are close to each other, with our calibrated curve being more smooth. We also report</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the values of ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> of the calibrated distribution, which we call the calibrated ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, and compare it</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">with empirical ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The left panel of Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> shows the curves of empirical and calibrated ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In the right panel of Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we create a scatter plot using empirical and calibrated ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Both</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ﬁgures show that the empirical and calibrated ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> curves are quite close.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">To further enrich the theory of PELVE, we study a few technical properties of PELVE, such as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">monotonicity and convergence as the probability level goes to 0. A decreasing PELVE indicates a</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 1: Empirical VaR and calibrated VaR</span>











<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 2: Empirical ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and calibrated ES</span>




















<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">relatively larger impact of ES in risk assessment than VaR moving towards the tail. As we will see,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">while for the most known parametric distributions the PELVE is decreasing, there exist some examples</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">at some risk levels it is not decreasing. This means that for those examples VaR becomes a stricter</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">risk measure when moving towards the tail.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">To obtain conditions for monotonicity, we deﬁne the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">dual PELVE by moving the multiplier</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from the ES side to the VaR side. PELVE can be seen as a</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">functional measure of tail heaviness in the sense that a heavier-tailed distribution has a higher PELVE</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">curve (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Theorem 1)). The hazard rate, on the other hand, is another functional</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">measure of tail heaviness. We show that the PELVE is decreasing (increasing) if the inverse of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">hazard rate is convex (concave). Monotonicity also leads to conditions for the PELVE to have a limit</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">at the tail, which from the risk management perspective, identiﬁes the ultimate relative positions of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES and VaR in the tail region. From a mathematical perspective, the limit of PELVE at 0 allows us</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">to extend the domain of PELVE to include 0 as a measure of tail heaviness.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> introduces the background and examples</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of the PELVE. In Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> we calibrate a distribution from ﬁnitely many points in the PELVE curve.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> calibrates the distribution from given PELVE curves, where we give a class of explicit solutions</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for constant PELVE functions and numerical solutions for general PELVE functions. In Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">study the monotonicity and convergence of the PELVE. Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> presents two examples of the model</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">calibration techniques applied to datasets used in insurance. A conclusion is given in Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Some</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">technical proofs of results in Sections</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are provided in the Appendices.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Deﬁnitions and background</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Let us consider an atomless probability space (Ω</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the set of the measurable sets</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the probability measure. Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">be the set of integrable random variables, i.e.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">=</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> {</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> :</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">|</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">|</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the expectation with respect to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We ﬁrst deﬁne VaR and ES in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the two most popular risk measures. The VaR at probability</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">level</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) is deﬁned as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> :</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the distribution of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The ES at probability level</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) is deﬁned as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">p</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">q,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Note that we use the “small</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> α</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">” convention for VaR and ES, which is diﬀerent from</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Liu and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2021</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Let VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = ess-inf(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). We have that ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the mean of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We will also call</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) the quantile function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, keeping in mind that in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">our convention this function is decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1), the PELVE at level</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, proposed by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), is deﬁned as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> {</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">] : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">where inf(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∅</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) used Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for our Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), and our choice of notation is due</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">to the fact that the curve</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> →</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is the main quantity of interest in this paper.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The PELVE of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is ﬁnite if and only if VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]. The value of the PELVE is the multiplier</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> If VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is not a constant for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">], then the PELVE is the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">unique solution for the multiplier. By Theorem 1 in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), the PELVE is location-scale</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">invariant. The distribution with a heavy tail will have a higher PELVE value.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a normal distributed random variable and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1%, we have Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ≈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5. It means that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ≈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). That is, the replacement suggested by BCBS is fair for normally distributed</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">risks. In other words, a higher PELVE will result in a higher capital requirement after the replacement.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this paper, we are generally interested in the question of which distributions have a speciﬁed</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">or partially speciﬁed PELVE curve. We ﬁrst look at a few simple examples.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (Constant PELVE)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> We ﬁrst list some distributions that have constant PELVE curves.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">From the deﬁnition of the PELVE, we know that the PELVE should be larger than 1. As we can</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">see from Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the PELVE for the generalized Pareto distribution takes values on (1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> GPD(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), we have 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0, Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0 and Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> > e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> when</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0. Furthermore, if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> follows the point-mass distribution</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> or the Bernoulli distribution, we have</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Table 1: Example of constant PELVE</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Distribution</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Distribution or probability function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1 for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">B(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> P</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1 for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 2 for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">EXP(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> exp(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λt</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/e</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">GPD(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (1 +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξx</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ̸</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> exp(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>







<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 2.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Here we present some non-constant PELVE examples. We write t(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">v</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for the t-distribution</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">with parameter (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, v</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), and LN(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for the log-normal distribution with parameter (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). As we can</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">see, for normal distribution and t-distribution, the PELVE curve is decreasing as</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> increasing. The</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">monotonicity of the PELVE of the lognormal distribution depends on the value of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The monotonicity</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of the PELVE will be further discussed in Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. For more PELVE examples, see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 3: PELVE for normal distribution, t-distribution and lognormal distribution</span>


































<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">3</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Calibration from ﬁnite-point constraints</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this section, we discuss the calibration problem when some points of the PELVE are given. We</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">will focus on the case where one point or two points on the PELVE curve are speciﬁed, for which we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">can explicitly construct a corresponding quantile function.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We ﬁrst note that the calibrated distribution is not unique. For example, if we are given Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">01) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5, we can assume the distribution of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the Normal distribution or the generalized Pareto distri-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">bution with tail parameter</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying (1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5 from Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Therefore, the distributions</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">obtained in our results are only some possible choices, which we choose to have a generalized Pareto</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">tail, as Pareto tails are standard in risk management applications.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">3.1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Calibration from a one-point constraint</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Based on Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can calibrate the distribution for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from one given PELVE point (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">such that Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. A simple idea is to take the generalized Pareto distribution when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1 and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1. We summarize the idea in the following Proposition.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition 1.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Then,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> GPD</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ξ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> has</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for some constant</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">has</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">7</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The proof can be directly derived from Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and it is omitted. By Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, if we have</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the value of PELVE at point</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can ﬁnd a distribution of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> which has the same PELVE value at</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. If we also have the value of VaR at</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can determine the scale parameter (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for the GPD</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">distribution or the value of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> to match the value of VaR. For Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can see that the calibrated</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">generalized Pareto distribution can also serve as a solution for a more prudent condition Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">3.2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Calibration from a two-point constraint</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The calibration problem would be much more diﬃcult when we are given two points of the PELVE</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">curve. Given two points (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we want to ﬁnd a distribution for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">such that Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Nevertheless, the choices of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) are</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">not arbitrary. First, we need 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> by the deﬁnition of the PELVE. Then,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we will show that the value of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> will be restricted if (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are given.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Lemma 1.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Then, we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">By Lemma</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, for given</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the value of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is bounded below by both 1 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">also note that if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1, then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is constant on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), which implies</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1. In Appendix</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> shows that the above lower bound is achieved if and only if VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">From the deﬁnition of the PELVE and Lemma</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the possible choices of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) should satisfy 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We denote by ∆ the admissible</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">set for (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), that is,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∆ =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> {</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ((0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ×</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">:</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We illustrate the possible region of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) with given</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> in Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We divide the region into</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5 cases and calibrate the distribution for each case.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The calibration process is to construct a continuous and decreasing quantile function that can</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">satisfy two equivalent conditions between VaR and ES, which are</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(2)</span>
