<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we show that a strictly decreasing quantile function cannot attain the boundary cases (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">),</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and hence the ﬂat parts are necessary to include and unify these cases.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We can easily get the distribution of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). As the PELVE is scale-location invariant,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we can scale or move the distribution we get to match more information. For example, if VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) are given, we can choose two constants</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> µ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λX</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> µ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> matches the speciﬁed</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR values. In a similar spirit, the calibration problem can be extended to calibrate the distributions</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">from some given ES and VaR values. The two points calibration problem can be regarded as given</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">two ES and VaR values. Calibrating from only ES or VaR would be easy. However, the choices of ES</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">values will also be limited by VaR values if we consider them at the same time, which is the same as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the choice of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> as we discussed in this section.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">11</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">3.3</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Calibration from an n-point constraint</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As we see above, the PELVE calibration problem is quite technical even when only two points on</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the PELVE curve are given. By extending the constraint to more than two points, the problem will in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">general become much more complicated. We brieﬂy discuss this problem in this section.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point constraint problem, we ﬁrst need to ﬁgure out the admissible set for (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">By Lemma</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the admissible set for the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point calibration problem is a subset of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> : 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> · · ·</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, . . . , c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> . . .</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">However, it is not clear whether each point in the above set is admissible. There are other constraints</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for the admissible points such as Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Once the admissible set is determined, we need to</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">divide the admissible set according to the position of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, . . . , n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Furthermore, the case</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i, j</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, . . . , n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> need special attention as Cases 1, 2 and 3 in the two-point</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">constraint problem. For instance, in the three-point constraint problem, we need to discuss over 10</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">separate cases.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Below, we only discuss some special cases of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. First, if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1, then the problem</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">becomes trivial, as the calibrated quantile functions satisfy VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ˆ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for some </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ˆ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> in [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">].</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For the case</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> > ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, . . . , n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can set the calibrated quantile function in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">] recursively. This is because such a conﬁguration of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> allows for separation of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">constraints, in the sense that we can adjust the values of VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">] to match PELVE at</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> without disturbing VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Let VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) be the calibrated quantile function from</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point constraint problem for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, . . . , n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> where VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) follows Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The calibrated</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">quantile function for the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point constraint problem is</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">where</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">+</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In particular, for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 3, and assuming</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> > ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the calibrated function is given by, with</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">12</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∆,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">where</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"></span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) + 2(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">+</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we show the calibrated quantile function for the case (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">005</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">025</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1) and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5). Note that the condition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is needed here.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 6: Calibrated quantile function when (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">005</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">025</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5)</span>





<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Although we cannot solve the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point constraint problem in general, we can instead discuss cali-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">bration from a given PELVE curve, which is the problem addressed in the next section.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">4</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Calibration from a curve constraint</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">By the location-scale invariance properties of the PELVE, we know that the solution cannot be</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">unique. Conversely, it would be interesting to ask whether all solutions can be linearly transformed</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">from a particular solution; that is, for a given function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), whether the set</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> {</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> : Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a location-scale class. This question, as well as identifying</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = Π, is the main objective</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of this section.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">4.1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">PELVE and dual PELVE</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">First, we note that calibrated distributions from an entire PELVE curve</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) would</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">be unnatural, because the existence of the PELVE requires</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) which may not hold for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">13</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">not very small. Thus, the PELVE curve Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> does not behave well on some parts of (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1). To address</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">this issue, we introduce a new notion called the dual PELVE and an integral equation which can help</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">us to calibrate the distribution by diﬀerential equations. The dual PELVE is deﬁned by moving the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">multiplier in PELVE from the ES side to the VaR side.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Deﬁnition 1.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the dual PELVE function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> at level</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] is deﬁned as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1 : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The existence and uniqueness of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) can be shown in the same way as the existence and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">uniqueness of the PELVE. There are advantages and disadvantages of working with both notions; see</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Remark 2). In our context, the main advantage of using the dual PELVE is that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is ﬁnite for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1], while Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is ﬁnite only when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Note that for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with a discontinuous quantile function, there may not exist</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). In order to guarantee the above equivalence, we make the following assumption for the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">quantile function, represented by general function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Assumption 1.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> The function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is strictly decreasing and continuous, and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">|</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">|</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the set of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> L</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The requirement that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the quantile function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is continuous and strictly decreasing is equivalent to that the distribution</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">function is continuous and strictly increasing in (ess-inf(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)); see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts and Hofert</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2013</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). We limit our discussion to random variables</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which include the most common models</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in risk management.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition 2.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we have</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε/π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Furthermore,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">unique solution</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> to the equation</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">It is straightforward to verify Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. By Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can calibrate the distribution</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">functions from dual PELVE instead of PELVE, and the calibrated distributions should satisfy the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">equation ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">4.2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">An integral equation associated with dual PELVE</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In order to calibrate distributions from the dual PELVE, we can equivalently focus on quantile</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">functions. Let us consider</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Then, solving</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is the same as solving</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">14</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in following equation:</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> yf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">zy</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(3)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The solution is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). As</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">),</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisﬁes Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Denote by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> C</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the set of all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ C</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the existence of the solution</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is guaranteed by</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the mean-value theorem and its uniqueness is obvious. For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1], let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) be the solution to (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">associated with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Clearly,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> yz</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is strictly increasing. This is similar to Lemma</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for the two-point case. Obviously,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is also location-scale invariant under linear transformation</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ C</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. That is,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λ ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Furthermore,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is continuous as</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is continuous</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and strictly decreasing. The next proposition is a simple connection between</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition 3.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for some</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> has the dual</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is solution to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, there exists</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for some</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and the solution to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Hence,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a continuous and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">strictly increasing distribution function and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1). Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. As</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Take</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Then, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1]. Furthermore, we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Therefore, there exists</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Then, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1).</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> allows us to study</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> instead of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for the calibration problem. The integral equation</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) can be very helpful in characterizing the distribution from the dual PELVE.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Some examples of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are listed in Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which is corresponding to the PELVE presented</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Table 2: Example of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span>
