<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6. Finally, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> on [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">].</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Note that since we start with a strictly decreasing function, then from equation (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) we have</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">so</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> remains strictly decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The solution produced by the numerical method heavily relies on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The equation (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) does</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">not have a unique solution, but the solution from the above process is unique. We set</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> as (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) by</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">assuming</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> can be extended from (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and set</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1. We use this assumption</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for simpliﬁcation as we can know that (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) satisﬁes (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for a constant</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4.4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. This choice of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the same as the choice of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) in the two-point calibration problem, and this reﬂects our subjective</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">view of the importance of the Pareto distribution in risk management. Especially, when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">19</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">some constant</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> u</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x/c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Therefore, (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) gives</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">w</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">If we set</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> as (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), we can have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> also in the form of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Then, it is obvious that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is also in the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">form of (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Therefore, the numerical method gives the simplest power function or logarithm function</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is a constant on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] as Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which leads to the generalized Pareto distribution for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">4.6</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Numerical calibrated quantile function</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Now let us explore the method in Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4.5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with simulation. Here we present the results for a</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">few cases. In Figures</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 8</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 11</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we compare the solution from the numerical method with the standard</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">formula in Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> in the left panel, and compare</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> yf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) to validate the equation</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) in the right panel.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We ﬁrst try some examples where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is constant as shown in Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, i.e.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2 (Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 8</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">),</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 9</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">9</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 10</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). For Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 8</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 10</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can see that the numerical</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">method provides exactly the same function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> as Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 11</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we check the case</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = log (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> /x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">satisﬁes</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We can see that the solution from the numerical method is close to a function of the form</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λe</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">+</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> b</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which is known to satisfy the integral equation.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 8: Calibrated function and validation for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span>
























<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">5</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Technical properties of the PELVE</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We now take a turn to study several additional properties of PELVE. In particular, we will obtain</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">results on the monotonicity and convergence of the dual PELVE as well as the PELVE.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">20</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 9: Calibrated function and validation for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/e</span>




















<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 10: Calibrated function and validation for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">9</span>















<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">5.1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Basic properties of dual PELVE</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The following proposition that shows the PELVE and dual PELVE share some basic properties</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">such as monotonicity (i), location-scale invariance (ii) and shape relevance (iii)-(iv) below.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition 4.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Suppose the quantile function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> satisﬁes Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(i)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is increasing (decreasing) in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> if and only if so is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(ii) For all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λ ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> a</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(iii)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for all strictly increasing concave functions:</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> :</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> →</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(iv)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for all strictly increasing convex functions:</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> g</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> :</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> →</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> g</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The statements (ii)-(iv) are parallel to the corresponding statements in Theorem 1 of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) on PELVE. The proof of Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is put in Appendix</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> C</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> allows us to study</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">21</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 11: Calibrated function and validation for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = log (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> /x</span>














<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the monotonicity and convergence of the PELVE by analyzing the corresponding properties of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">dual PELVE, which is more convenient in many cases. In the following sections, we focus on ﬁnding</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the conditions which make the dual PELVE monotone and convergent at 0. By Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, those</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">conditions can also apply to the PELVE.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">5.2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Non-monotone and non-convergent examples</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this section, we study the monotonicity and convergence of dual PELVE. For monotonicity, we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">have shown some well-known distributions such as normal distribution, t-distribution and lognormal</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">distribution have monotone PELVE curves in Example</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. However, the PELVE is not monotone for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Below we provide an example.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (Non-monotone PELVE)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Let us consider the following density function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> g</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> on [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2],</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">g</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> + 2)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> + (2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with density function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> g</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 12</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> presents the value of Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5). As one can see,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the PELVE is not necessarily decreasing, and so is the dual PELVE.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For the convergence, it is clear that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is continuous in (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Therefore, lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">exists for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1). However, both Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) are not well deﬁned at</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0. If lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">exists, we can deﬁne</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(0) as the limit, and Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(0) similarly. However, the following example shows</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">that the limit does not exist for some</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (No limit at 0)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> We can construct a random variable</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> such that lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) does</span>

<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">5.3</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Suﬃcient condition for monotonicity and convergence</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In risk management applications, for a random variable</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> modeling a random loss, the behavior</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of its tail is the most important. Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">be the upper</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-tail distribution of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (see e.g.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Liu and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2021</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)), namely</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We will see that the dual PELVE of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is a part of the dual PELVE of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Lemma 2.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the distribution function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, it holds</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> It is clear that VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Therefore,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1 : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1 : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Thus, we have the desired result.</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The tail distribution can provide a condition to check whether the dual PELVE is decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition 5.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the distribution function of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is convex (concave) for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are decreasing</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(increasing).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1), let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. By Lemma</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)). Furthermore,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we have</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">((1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">+</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> U(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1),</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">U</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We assume that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is a convex function on (ess-inf(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) ﬁrst.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> :</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> →</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> R</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be a strictly increasing convex function such that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">((1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (ess-inf(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Then, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">=</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). By Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we get</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). As</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)), we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1). Thus,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is decreasing. By</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proposition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we have Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is also decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">On the other hand, if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is concave, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for all</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is increasing. So is Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">24</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The condition</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is convex (concave) for all</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) is generally hard to</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">check. Intuitively, this condition means that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">has a less heavy tail compared to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We can further</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">simplify this condition by using the hazard rate function. For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈ X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with distribution function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">density function</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the survival function and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f/S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the hazard rate function.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is continuous and strictly increasing,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is continuous and strictly decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Theorem 3.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the hazard rate function</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is second-order diﬀerentiable and convex (concave), then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are decreasing</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(increasing).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The proof of Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is provided in Appendix</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> C</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 6.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For the normal distribution, we can give a short proof of the convexity of 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> S</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">be the survival function of the standard normal distribution and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> its density. Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = exp</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">� �</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">exp</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. One can easily see that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> xI</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(7)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">which gives</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> xI</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). This with (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) implies that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1 +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(8)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">First, consider the negative line i.e.,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0. In this case (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">8</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) imply</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> xI</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (1 +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) + (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> The implication of the two relations is that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a convex and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">decreasing function on negative line. Now we consider the case</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0. In this case, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">From what we have proved it is clear that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is an increasing function on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0. On the other hand, we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">√</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> exp</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. This combined with (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) gives us</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) +</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">√</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> exp</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">+</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> i</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> , x ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">This means</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is an increasing function on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0 as it is a summation of two other increasing functions,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">so</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> I</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is convex on the positive line as well.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 13</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> presents the curve 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for the generalized Pareto distribution, the Normal distribution,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the t-distribution and the Lognormal distribution. For distributions GPD(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2), N(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) and t(2), we</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">can see that the curves 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are convex, and this coincides with decreasing PELVE shown in Example</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For the Lognormal distribution, the shape of 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> depends on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. As shown in Example</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, the PELVE</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for LN(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is visibly decreasing for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">04 and increasing for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1. Corresponding to the above</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">observations, we see that 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is convex for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">04 and concave for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= 1.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">25</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure 13: 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for GPD(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2), N(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1), t(2), LN(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2) and LN(1) in blue curves; in the right panel, the</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">red curve is linear</span>




































<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Corollary 1.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> If the hazard rate of a random variable</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is second-order diﬀerentiable and concave,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are decreasing.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Just note that if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is concave, then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ηη</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is non-positive. It follows that</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span>
