<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">=</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">−</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2 (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ηη</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Thus, 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is convex, and the desired statement follows from Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The corollary above is a result of the fact that the concavity of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> implies convexity of 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Therefore, concave</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> always leads to decreasing PELVE. For example, the Gamma distribution G(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">α, λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">with density</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> λ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Γ(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">α</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) has concave hazard rate function when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> α ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1. Furthermore, by</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can easily ﬁnd more well-known distributions that have decreasing</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As the tail distribution determines</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> around 0, we can focus on the tail distribution to discuss</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the convergence of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> at 0. Note that if the survival distribution function is regularly varying, then its</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">tail parameter one-to-one corresponds to the limit of Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> at 0 as shown by Theorem 3 of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Hence, the limit of Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, if it exists, can be useful as a measure of tail heaviness, and it is well</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">deﬁned even for distributions that do not have a heavy tail. By the monotone convergence theorem,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we have lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) exists if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is monotone. The limit may be ﬁnite or inﬁnite.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Corollary 2.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with quantile function satisfying Assumption</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> be the hazard rate of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. If</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is second-order diﬀerentiable and convex (concave) in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for some</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">then</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> exists. In particular, this is true if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is second-order diﬀerentiable and concave on</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">∼</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Then, the survival function for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The density function is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Therefore, the hazard rate function is</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/S</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">26</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">As 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is convex (concave) when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> x > F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), we have 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">x</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is convex (concave).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">By</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is decreasing (increasing) on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1). As a result, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is decreasing</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(increasing) on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) exists.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">By Corollary</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is concave on (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)), 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/η</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is convex on (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">δ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ess-sup(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">))</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) also exists.</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Example 7.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> If lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is a constant, we have lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = lim</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) as</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). We give the numerical values of Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) at very small probability levels</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> for normal, t, and log-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">normal distributions. These distributions do not have a constant PELVE curve, and using Corollary</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we can check that their PELVE have limits. As we can see from Table</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, PELVE can still distinguish</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the heaviness of the tail even when</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is very small. The heavier tailed distributions report a higher</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE value. For the normal distribution and the log-normal distribution with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> σ</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2, the value of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE is close to</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ≈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">7183 as</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ↓</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0. From the numerical values, it is unclear whether Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> →</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for all log-normal distributions, but there is no practical relevance to compute Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 10</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">applications.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Table 3: Values of Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Distribution</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">N</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">LN(1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">LN(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">LN(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t(2)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t(3)</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 10</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.6884</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.9167</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.7944</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.7290</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4.0000</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3.3750</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 10</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.6909</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.9077</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.7920</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2.7287</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">4.0000</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3.3750</span>


<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">6</span>
<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Applications to datasets used in insurance</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this section, we apply the PEVLE calibration techniques to datasets used in insurance to show</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">how to use the calibrated distribution in estimating risk measure values and simulation.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">6.1</span>
<span  style="font-size: 11.9552001953125px;">Dental expenditure data</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this example, we apply the calibration model to the 6494 complete household component’s</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">total dental expenditure data from Medical Expenditure Panel Survey for 2020. An earlier version of</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the same dataset is used by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Behan et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2010</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) to study the relationship between worker absenteeism</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and overweight or obesity. The main purpose of this experiment is to construct tractable models, with</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">continuous and simple quantile functions, which have similar risk measure values as the original dataset,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and the same PELVE at certain levels. We present in Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 14</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> two quantile functions calibrated from</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), with (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">01</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1), respectively.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">The two</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">calibrated quantile functions are scaled up according to the empirical VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). By</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">27</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we can calibrate the quantile functions from Case 4 when (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">01</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05), and from</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Case 5 when (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1). As mentioned before, for (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1), we set the calibrated</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">quantile function in (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) as the Pareto quantile function. Hence, there is no ﬂat part in the two</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">calibrated quantile functions shown in Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 14</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. As we can see, both the two calibrated quantile</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">functions ﬁt the empirical quantile functions well. The calibrated quantile function can be regarded as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a special parameterized model for tail distribution, which can ﬁt the value of VaR and ES at speciﬁed</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">levels. With the parameterized calibrated model, we can estimate the value of tail risk measures (see</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Liu and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2021</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)) such as ES, VaR, and Range-VaR (RVaR), amongst others. In Tables</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">we compute the values of ES and RVaR for the calibrated model and compare them with empirical ES</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">and RVaR values, respectively, where the risk measure RVaR is deﬁned as</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">RVaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">β</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> −</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> α</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">γ</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> α < β <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1; see</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Cont et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2010</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2018</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). As we scale the calibrated quan-</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">tile function to empirical VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), the calibrated ES and empirical ES are identical at</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">levels</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) by the deﬁnition of PELVE. For other probability levels, the calibrated</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES and RVaR in Tables</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> are close to their empirical counterparts. When (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">01</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05),</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">it may only be useful to compute calibrated ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05) = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">11591 because the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">calibrated quantile function is arbitrary beyond the level 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">11591. If we need to estimate ES or RVaR</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">for a larger probability level, we can choose a higher</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> as long as</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is satisﬁed. For</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">this dataset, the highest</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> we can use is 0.1983.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Using the methods in Section</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 3</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, for quantile levels between (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), the distribution calibrated</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">from one point (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is the same as the one calibrated from two points (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). Hence,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the results for ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> of the one-point calibrated function are also shown in Tables</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 4</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 5</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> in the cells</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Calibrated RVaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">01</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5003.7</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3360.2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2160.6</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Calibrated RVaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> from (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">5634.6</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">3561.8</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1983.1</span>


<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(NIS-HCUP). The data contains 500 hospital costs observations with 244 males and 256 females which</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">can be regarded as the losses of the health insurance policies.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Using the calibration model of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">two-point constraint problem, we calibrate quantile functions for females and males from PELVE at</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">probability levels</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1, which are shown in Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 15</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Except for estimating the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">risk measure, the calibrated distribution is useful in simulation. Assume the insurance company wants</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">to know the top 10% hospital costs; that is</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">|</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X ></span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the hospital costs. There</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">are only 24 available data for males and 25 available data for females, which would be not enough for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">making statistically solid decisions. To generate more pseudo-data points, we can simulate data from</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the calibrated distribution; that is, we simulate data from</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">where</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is the calibrated distribution</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 15</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. Taking</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> p</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">9, we have</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> F</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) with VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) from Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 15</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. We</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">simulate 1000 data from the calibrated distributions based on PELVE at</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">05 and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1. In</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 16</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, we present two QQ plots of simulated data against empirical data for females and males</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">respectively. As we can see, the simulated data has a similar distribution as the empirical data. Those</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">simulated pseudo-data points can be used for estimating risk measures or making other decisions. For</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">example, the simulated hospital cost can be used to design health insurance contrasts or set the premium</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">29</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">in complex systems, where sometimes methods based on simulated data are more convenient to work</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">with than methods relying on distribution functions. This may be seen as an alternative, smoothed,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">version of bootstrap; recall that the classic bootstrap sample can only take the values represented in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the dataset. Furthermore, we compare the simulated data of hospital costs for females and males in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Figure</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 17</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which shows that the distribution of the hospital costs for females has a heavier tail than</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">that for males.</span>

<span  style="font-size: 14.346199989318848px;">Conclusion</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In this paper, we oﬀer several contributions to the calibration problem and properties of the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">PELVE. The calibration problem concerns, with some given values from a PELVE curve, how one can</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">build a distribution that has this PELVE. We solve a few settings of calibration based on a one-point</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">constraint, a two-point constraint, or the entire curve constraint. In particular, the calibration for a</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">given PELVE curve involves solving an integral equation</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">�</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> </span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">f</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) d</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">s</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> yf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> y</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) for a given function</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, and this requires some advanced analysis and a numerical method in diﬀerential equations. For</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">the case that</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> z</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a constant curve, we can identify all solutions, which are surprisingly complicated.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">In addition, we see that if</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> is a constant larger than</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> e</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, which is observed from typical values in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ﬁnancial return data (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)),</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> share the same tail behavior with the corresponding</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Pareto solution. We also applied our calibration techniques to two datasets used in insurance.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">On the technical side, we study whether the PELVE is monotone and whether it converges at 0.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">We show that the monotonicity of the PELVE is associated with the shape of the hazard rate. If the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">inverse of the hazard rate is convex (concave), the PELVE is decreasing (increasing). The monotonicity</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">at the tail part of the PELVE leads to conditions to check the convergence of the PELVE at 0. If the</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">inverse of the hazard rate is convex (concave) at the tail of the distribution, the limit of the PELVE</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">at 0 exists.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">There are several open questions related to PELVE that we still do not fully understand. One</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">particular such question is whether the tail behavior, e.g., tail index, of a distribution is completely</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">determined by its PELVE. We have seen that this holds true in the case of a constant PELVE (see</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">31</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), but we do not have a general conclusion. In the case of regularly varying survival functions,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, Theorem 3) showed that the limit of PELVE determines its tail parameter, but</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">it is unclear whether this can be generalized to other distributions. Another challenging task is, for</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">a speciﬁed curve</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> on [0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1], to determine whether there exists a model</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> with</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. The case</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">of</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">-point constraints for large</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> n</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> may require a new design of veriﬁcation algorithms. This question</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">concerns the compatibility of given information with statistical models, which has been studied, in</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">other applications of risk management, by</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Embrechts et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2002</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 2016</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Krause et al.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2018</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof of Lemma</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> As</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). By Proposition</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1 in</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Li and Wang</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2022</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> <</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∞</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1) satisfying</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> E</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">[</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">]</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">),</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">] : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">εc</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">= inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">{</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Let</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> {</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> [</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1] : ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">}</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. For any</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), we have 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> > ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> and</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Hence,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> k</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) and this gives</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⊆</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Therefore,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩾</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> inf</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> A</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>




<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Proof of Theorem</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> We will check the equivalent condition (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) between VaR and ES. Note that if</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is a constant on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), then Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1. If</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> �→</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is not a constant on (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), then</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is the unique solution that satisﬁes ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(i) Case 1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1. It is clear that VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is a constant for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">] and (</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">2</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is satisﬁed. Hence,</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">.</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> Moreover, VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is also a constant for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">], which implies Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1.</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(ii) Case 2</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">,</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> = 1 and 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> < c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ⩽</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> 1</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">/ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">. For</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">), VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) =</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> G</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is a constant for</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> t</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> ∈</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;"> (0</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">, c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">).</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">Hence, Π</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = 1. Next, we check whether ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) = VaR</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">). The value of ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">) is</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ES</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">(</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">X</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">)</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">=</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">1</span>

<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">c</span><span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">ε</span>
<span  style="font-size: 9.962639808654785px;">��</span>
